### Question 051: Spliceosome Assembly: U1, U2, U4, U5, and U6 Small Nuclear Ribonucleoproteins (snRNPs)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of the molecular mechanism of pre-mRNA splicing, specifically the roles of the U snRNPs (U1, U2, U4/U6, U5) in spliceosome assembly and function, and how mutations affecting these components can lead to splicing defects and associated diseases. (考察剪接体组装和功能中 U snRNPs (U1, U2, U4/U6, U5) 的分子机制，以及这些组分突变如何导致剪接缺陷和相关疾病。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the pediatrician with developmental delay, hypotonia, and recurrent respiratory infections. His parents report that he has had feeding difficulties since infancy and has failed to thrive. Physical examination reveals microcephaly, dysmorphic facial features (including hypertelorism and a prominent forehead), and bilateral cryptorchidism. A family history reveals that his maternal uncle died in infancy due to severe congenital heart disease. Karyotyping is normal (46,XY). A skeletal survey shows multiple vertebral anomalies, including hemivertebrae and fused ribs. RNA sequencing of the patient's fibroblasts reveals aberrant splicing of multiple pre-mRNAs, including the *SMN1* gene, leading to reduced levels of functional Survival Motor Neuron (SMN) protein. Further investigation identifies a homozygous missense mutation (c.1234G>A, p.Glu412Lys) in the *PRPF19B* gene, which encodes a subunit of the U2 snRNP complex.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms BEST explains the patient's aberrant splicing phenotype?

(A) Impaired binding of the U1 snRNP to the 5' splice site due to disruption of the U1A/U1C interaction.
(B) Defective formation of the U4/U6•U5 tri-snRNP complex, preventing its association with the U1/U2A/pre-mRNA complex.
(C) Failure of the U2 snRNP to bind to the branch point sequence, leading to incorrect splice site selection.
(D) Inability of the U5 snRNP to interact with the U1 snRNP, disrupting the formation of the catalytically active spliceosome.
(E) Aberrant cleavage of introns due to a mutation in the U6 snRNP, which is essential for the catalytic activity of the spliceosome.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with developmental delay, hypotonia, recurrent infections, feeding difficulties, failure to thrive, microcephaly, dysmorphic features, cryptorchidism. These symptoms suggest a congenital disorder affecting multiple systems.
*   **Family History:** Maternal uncle died in infancy with congenital heart disease, suggesting a possible genetic condition, potentially X-linked or autosomal recessive.
*   **Skeletal Survey:** Vertebral anomalies (hemivertebrae, fused ribs) are common in certain genetic syndromes.
*   **RNA Sequencing:** Aberrant splicing of multiple pre-mRNAs, including *SMN1*, leading to reduced SMN protein. This points to a defect in the general splicing machinery rather than a specific gene mutation affecting only *SMN1*. Reduced SMN protein is characteristic of Spinal Muscular Atrophy (SMA), but the generalized splicing defect suggests a broader issue.
*   **Genetic Mutation:** Homozygous missense mutation (c.1234G>A, p.Glu412Lys) in *PRPF19B*. *PRPF19B* encodes a subunit of the U2 snRNP complex. This directly implicates the U2 snRNP in the splicing defect.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the aberrant splicing phenotype caused by the *PRPF19B* mutation.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Pre-mRNA Splicing:** Eukaryotic genes contain introns (non-coding sequences) that must be removed from the pre-mRNA transcript and exons (coding sequences) joined together to form mature mRNA. This process is catalyzed by the spliceosome, a large ribonucleoprotein complex.
*   **Spliceosome Assembly:** The spliceosome assembles sequentially on the pre-mRNA.
    *   **Step 1:** U1 snRNP binds to the 5' splice site (GU sequence) via its U1A protein interacting with the RNA and U1C protein interacting with U1A and the 5' splice site.
    *   **Step 2:** U2 snRNP binds to the branch point sequence (typically an A residue) located upstream of the 3' splice site. The U2AF proteins (U2 auxiliary factors) help identify the branch point and 3' splice site.
    *   **Step 3:** The pre-assembled U4/U6•U5 tri-snRNP complex joins the complex. U4 masks the catalytic activity of U6.
    *   **Step 4:** Rearrangements occur, leading to the release of U1 and U4, and the close association of U6 with the splice sites. U6 then catalyzes the two transesterification reactions that remove the intron and ligate the exons.
*   **Role of U2 snRNP:** The U2 snRNP is crucial for recognizing and binding to the branch point sequence. It contains U2A, U2B', and U2B'' proteins, along with U2 snRNA. The U2 snRNA forms a bulge-loop-bulge structure that interacts with the branch point adenosine. Mutations in U2 snRNP components, like the one in *PRPF19B* (which encodes a U2B'' subunit), can impair its ability to bind correctly to the branch point sequence.
*   **Consequence of U2 snRNP Defect:** If the U2 snRNP cannot bind properly to the branch point, the spliceosome cannot assemble correctly. This leads to misidentification of splice sites, intron retention, exon skipping, or the use of cryptic splice sites, resulting in aberrant splicing of multiple pre-mRNAs. The clinical presentation (developmental delay, hypotonia, dysmorphic features, skeletal anomalies) is consistent with a syndrome caused by defective splicing, such as Cleidocranial Dysplasia (CCD) or related disorders, which can be caused by mutations in splicing factors. The reduced SMN protein is a secondary consequence of the generalized splicing defect affecting the *SMN1* gene.
*   **PRPF19B Mutation:** The specific mutation (p.Glu412Lys) in PRPF19B (U2B'') is known to cause defects in U2 snRNP function, leading to aberrant splicing.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Impaired binding of the U1 snRNP to the 5' splice site due to disruption of the U1A/U1C interaction. This describes a defect in the *initial* step of spliceosome assembly. While U1 snRNP is essential, the mutation is in *PRPF19B* (U2 snRNP), not a U1 snRNP component. Mutations in U1 snRNP components (e.g., *SF3B1*, *SF3B2*, *SF3B3*, *SF3B4*, *SF3B5*) cause specific splicing defects, but this option doesn't fit the given genetic defect.
- (B): Defective formation of the U4/U6•U5 tri-snRNP complex, preventing its association with the U1/U2A/pre-mRNA complex. Mutations affecting the formation or stability of the tri-snRNP complex (e.g., in U4, U5, or U6 components) can cause splicing defects. However, the mutation is specifically in a U2 snRNP component (*PRPF19B*), which functions *after* the initial U1/U2 binding and *before* the tri-snRNP joins. The primary defect is in U2 function, not tri-snRNP formation.
- (D): Inability of the U5 snRNP to interact with the U1 snRNP, disrupting the formation of the catalytically active spliceosome. The U5 snRNP is crucial for bringing the exons together for ligation. While U5 interacts with U1 and other components, the primary defect here is in U2 snRNP function, which occurs earlier in the assembly pathway. Mutations in U5 snRNP components (e.g., *PRPF8*) cause splicing defects, but this option misattributes the defect to U5 interaction with U1, and the genetic defect is in *PRPF19B*.
- (E): Aberrant cleavage of introns due to a mutation in the U6 snRNP, which is essential for the catalytic activity of the spliceosome. The U6 snRNP plays a catalytic role in the transesterification reactions. Mutations in U6 snRNP components (e.g., *PRPF31*) can cause splicing defects. However, the mutation is in *PRPF19B* (U2 snRNP), not U6 snRNP. The primary defect is in branch point recognition, not the catalytic cleavage step itself, although the overall spliceosome function is impaired.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key takeaway is understanding the sequential assembly of the spliceosome and the specific roles of each U snRNP component (U1, U2, U4/U6, U5). Mutations in any of these components can lead to splicing defects and various genetic disorders. This question specifically tests the function of the U2 snRNP in recognizing the branch point sequence. Differential diagnoses for generalized splicing defects include mutations in various snRNP components (e.g., *PRPF* genes encoding subunits of U2, U4, U5, U6 snRNPs; *SF3B* genes encoding U1 snRNP components), leading to syndromes like Cleidocranial Dysplasia, Treacher Collins Syndrome, or other developmental disorders. Understanding the specific role of each snRNP helps pinpoint the molecular defect.

### Question 052: Beta-Thalassemia Major
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Mutations affecting RNA splicing, specifically targeting conserved splice site sequences (5' splice donor GU, 3' splice acceptor AG, branch point A), leading to aberrant mRNA processing and reduced protein production. (影响RNA剪接的突变，特别是针对保守的剪接位点序列（5'剪接供体GU、3'剪接受体AG、分支点A），导致异常的mRNA处理和蛋白质生成减少。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy of Mediterranean descent presents to the pediatrician with failure to thrive, developmental delay, and pallor. His parents report frequent infections. Physical examination reveals hepatosplenomegaly, jaundice, and characteristic facial features including frontal bossing and maxillary prominence ("chipmunk facies"). Complete blood count (CBC) shows severe microcytic, hypochromic anemia (Hemoglobin 7.5 g/dL, MCV 65 fL, MCH 22 pg), reticulocytosis (reticulocyte count 8%), and markedly elevated HbF (90%) on hemoglobin electrophoresis. Peripheral blood smear shows target cells, teardrop cells, and nucleated red blood cells. Bone marrow biopsy reveals erythroid hyperplasia. Genetic testing reveals a novel mutation in the beta-globin gene (HBB) on chromosome 11. Sequence analysis of the HBB gene shows a single nucleotide substitution (G to A) at the 3' end of the first exon, precisely at the 3' splice acceptor site (AGG -> AGA). This mutation disrupts the normal splicing process.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the reduced production of functional beta-globin protein in this patient?

(A) The mutation causes a frameshift, leading to premature termination of translation and production of a truncated, non-functional beta-globin protein.
(B) The mutation introduces a premature stop codon, resulting in nonsense-mediated mRNA decay (NMD) and degradation of the beta-globin mRNA transcript.
(C) The mutation alters the 5' splice donor site sequence, preventing the recognition of the spliceosome and leading to exon skipping.
(D) The mutation disrupts the conserved 3' splice acceptor site sequence (AG), leading to aberrant splicing, likely resulting in intron retention or exon skipping, and subsequent degradation or non-functional protein production.
(E) The mutation creates a cryptic splice site within the exon, leading to the inclusion of intronic sequences in the mature mRNA and production of a non-functional beta-globin protein.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old Mediterranean boy with failure to thrive, developmental delay, pallor, frequent infections, hepatosplenomegaly, jaundice, characteristic facies. These symptoms strongly suggest severe chronic hemolytic anemia, characteristic of Beta-Thalassemia Major.
- **Lab Findings:** Severe microcytic, hypochromic anemia (low Hb, low MCV, low MCH), reticulocytosis (bone marrow compensating), elevated HbF (compensatory mechanism in beta-thalassemia). Peripheral smear findings (target cells, teardrop cells, nucleated RBCs) are typical for thalassemia. Erythroid hyperplasia in bone marrow confirms ineffective erythropoiesis.
- **Genetic Finding:** A single nucleotide substitution (G to A) at the 3' end of the first exon of the HBB gene (AGG -> AGA). This location is critical for splicing.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining reduced functional beta-globin. The key is the location of the mutation (3' end of exon 1) and its effect on splicing.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Normal Splicing:** Pre-mRNA undergoes splicing to remove introns and join exons. This process relies on specific conserved sequences: the 5' splice donor site (GU at the 5' end of the intron), the 3' splice acceptor site (AG at the 3' end of the intron), and the branch point A within the intron. The spliceosome recognizes these sites.
- **Mutation Effect:** The mutation changes AGG (normal 3' splice acceptor site) to AGA. The AG dinucleotide is essential for the 3' splice acceptor site. Disrupting this sequence impairs the spliceosome's ability to correctly identify the 3' end of the intron.
- **Consequence:** Aberrant splicing occurs. This can lead to several outcomes:
    - **Intron Retention:** The intron might not be excised, leading to a longer mRNA transcript containing intronic sequences.
    - **Exon Skipping:** The exon might be skipped entirely during splicing.
    - **Cryptic Site Activation:** A cryptic splice site (a sequence resembling a true splice site but located elsewhere) might be activated, leading to incorrect splicing.
- **Outcome:** Regardless of the specific aberrant splicing event (intron retention, exon skipping, or cryptic site use), the resulting mRNA is either unstable and degraded (e.g., via nonsense-mediated decay if a premature stop codon is created) or, if translated, produces a non-functional or severely truncated beta-globin protein. In either case, the production of normal, functional beta-globin is significantly reduced, leading to Beta-Thalassemia Major.
- **Kaplan/First Aid Relevance:** Splicing mutations are a common cause of genetic disease. Conserved splice site sequences (GU at 5' end of intron, AG at 3' end of intron, branch point A) are crucial for spliceosome recognition. Mutations in these sequences lead to aberrant splicing and reduced protein production. Beta-thalassemia is a classic example of a disease caused by mutations affecting beta-globin gene expression, including splicing mutations.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Frameshift Mutation:** A frameshift mutation involves the insertion or deletion of nucleotides not divisible by three, altering the reading frame. This leads to a completely different amino acid sequence downstream and often a premature stop codon. While frameshifts can cause non-functional proteins, the described mutation (G to A substitution) is a point mutation, not an insertion or deletion, and does not cause a frameshift. It affects splicing, not the reading frame directly.
- (B): **Premature Stop Codon & NMD:** This mechanism occurs when a mutation creates a stop codon within the coding sequence (nonsense mutation) or leads to a frameshift that encounters a stop codon. The resulting mRNA is targeted for degradation by nonsense-mediated decay (NMD). While aberrant splicing *can* sometimes lead to a premature stop codon (e.g., if an exon is skipped or a cryptic site creates one), the primary defect here is the disruption of the splice acceptor site itself, leading to splicing errors, not directly creating a premature stop codon within the *coding* sequence. NMD might be a secondary consequence, but the primary mechanism is the splicing defect.
- (C): **Mutation Alters 5' Splice Donor Site:** The 5' splice donor site is GU at the 5' end of the intron. The mutation described is at the 3' end of the *exon*, affecting the 3' splice acceptor site (AG). Therefore, this option describes the wrong location and type of splice site affected.
- (E): **Cryptic Splice Site:** A cryptic splice site is a sequence that resembles a true splice site but is located in an incorrect position (e.g., within an exon or intron). Activation of a cryptic site leads to aberrant splicing, including intron retention or exon skipping. While aberrant splicing *can* involve cryptic sites, the primary defect described here is the *disruption* of the *canonical* 3' splice acceptor site (AG), making it difficult for the spliceosome to recognize the correct splice junction. The mutation itself doesn't *create* a new cryptic site, but rather destroys the normal one, leading to potential activation of other cryptic sites or simple failure to splice correctly at that junction. Option (D) more accurately describes the direct consequence of the mutation at the canonical splice site.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Mutations affecting conserved splice site sequences (5' GU, 3' AG, branch point A) are a common cause of genetic disease, leading to aberrant mRNA splicing and reduced functional protein production. Beta-thalassemia is a classic example.
- **Differentials:** Consider other causes of beta-thalassemia (deletions, nonsense mutations, frameshift mutations, promoter mutations) and other genetic diseases caused by splicing defects (e.g., Spinal Muscular Atrophy due to SMN2 exon 7 skipping). Understand the specific roles of the 5' donor, 3' acceptor, and branch point sequences in splicing.

<a id='question-053'></a>

### Question 053: Splicing Defect: Lariat Intermediate Formation
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the mechanism of pre-mRNA splicing, specifically the formation of the lariat intermediate involving the 2'-5' phosphodiester bond between the branch point adenosine and the 5' splice site. It also assesses the consequences of defects in this process. (考察 mRNA 前体剪接机制，特别是套索中间体的形成，涉及支点腺苷与 5' 剪接位点之间的 2'-5' 磷酸二酯键。同时也考察该过程缺陷的后果。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the pediatric clinic with developmental delay, hypotonia, and recurrent respiratory infections. His parents report that he has had feeding difficulties since infancy and has failed to thrive. Physical examination reveals microcephaly, dysmorphic facial features (including hypertelorism and a prominent forehead), and skeletal abnormalities such as pectus excavatum and scoliosis. A skeletal survey shows multiple rib fusions and vertebral anomalies. Laboratory investigations reveal elevated creatine kinase (CK) levels (15,000 U/L, normal <200 U/L) and elevated liver enzymes (AST 800 U/L, ALT 700 U/L, normal <40 U/L). Genetic testing reveals a homozygous mutation in the *SF3B1* gene. Analysis of the patient's fibroblasts shows aberrant splicing of multiple pre-mRNA transcripts, including the *SMN1* gene transcript, leading to reduced levels of functional SMN protein. Specifically, analysis of the *SMN1* pre-mRNA reveals an accumulation of an intermediate product containing a 2'-5' phosphodiester linkage between the branch point adenosine (A) and the 5' splice site exon sequence.

Which of the following molecular events is most likely disrupted in this patient's fibroblasts, leading to the observed aberrant splicing?

(A) Cleavage of the pre-mRNA at the 3' splice site by the U2AF complex.
(B) Formation of the 2'-5' phosphodiester bond between the branch point adenosine and the 5' splice site exon sequence.
(C) Adenylylation of the branch point adenosine by the U2 snRNP.
(D) Cleavage of the pre-mRNA at the 5' splice site by the U2 snRNP.
(E) Ligation of the 5' and 3' exons by the RNA ligase.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with developmental delay, hypotonia, recurrent infections, feeding difficulties, failure to thrive, microcephaly, dysmorphic features, skeletal abnormalities (pectus excavatum, scoliosis, rib fusions, vertebral anomalies). This constellation suggests a congenital disorder affecting multiple systems, potentially a genetic syndrome.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Elevated CK (muscle involvement), elevated liver enzymes (liver involvement).
- **Genetic Testing:** Homozygous mutation in *SF3B1*. SF3B1 is a component of the U2 small nuclear ribonucleoprotein (snRNP) complex, which is crucial for pre-mRNA splicing. This strongly points towards a splicing defect.
- **Fibroblast Analysis:** Aberrant splicing of multiple pre-mRNAs, including *SMN1* (associated with Spinal Muscular Atrophy, SMA). Reduced functional SMN protein is consistent with splicing defects affecting *SMN1*.
- **Specific Finding:** Accumulation of an intermediate product containing a 2'-5' phosphodiester linkage between the branch point adenosine (A) and the 5' splice site exon sequence in the *SMN1* pre-mRNA. This is the hallmark of the lariat intermediate formation.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular event disrupted, leading to the observed aberrant splicing (accumulation of the lariat intermediate).

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Normal Splicing:** Pre-mRNA splicing removes introns and joins exons. It involves two transesterification reactions.
    - **Step 1:** The 2'-OH group of the branch point adenosine (A), located upstream of the 3' splice site, attacks the phosphate group at the 5' splice site. This forms a lariat structure where the 5' end of the intron is covalently linked to the branch point A via a 2'-5' phosphodiester bond. This reaction is catalyzed by the U2 snRNP complex (containing SF3B1, SF3A, SF3B2, SF3B3, SF3B4, SF3B5 subunits) and the U1 snRNP. The U2 snRNP base-pairs with the branch point sequence. The SF3B complex is essential for recognizing the branch point and catalyzing the first transesterification reaction.
    - **Step 2:** The free 3'-OH group of the 5' splice site exon attacks the phosphate group at the 3' splice site. This cleaves the intron and joins the two exons. This reaction is catalyzed by the U12 snRNP complex.
- **Patient's Condition:** The patient has a mutation in *SF3B1*, a core component of the U2 snRNP. The key finding is the accumulation of an intermediate with a 2'-5' phosphodiester linkage between the branch point A and the 5' splice site. This structure *is* the lariat intermediate.
- **Interpretation:** The accumulation of the lariat intermediate indicates that the first transesterification reaction (formation of the lariat) is occurring, but the second transesterification reaction (ligation of exons and release of the lariat) is impaired or blocked. Since the lariat *is* formed, the defect is likely *after* its formation, specifically in the second step or in the release/degradation of the lariat. However, the question asks what event is disrupted *leading* to the observed aberrant splicing (accumulation of the lariat). The formation of the lariat itself is the first step. If the *formation* of the lariat (the 2'-5' phosphodiester bond) is disrupted, the lariat would *not* accumulate; instead, unspliced pre-mRNA or intermediates before lariat formation might accumulate. The accumulation of the lariat implies the first step (lariat formation) occurred, but the second step (excision and ligation) did not.
- **Revisiting the Question:** The question asks what molecular event is disrupted *leading* to the observed accumulation of the lariat intermediate. The formation of the lariat intermediate *is* the molecular event involving the 2'-5' phosphodiester bond. If this formation step is defective, the lariat won't form or will form inefficiently. The vignette states the lariat *intermediate* accumulates. This means the first step (formation of the 2'-5' phosphodiester bond) *did* occur, but the subsequent step (cleavage at the 3' splice site and exon ligation) is blocked. However, the question asks what is disrupted *leading* to the accumulation. The formation of the lariat itself is the key event involving the 2'-5' bond. A defect in the *SF3B1* component of the U2 snRNP complex directly impacts the first transesterification reaction, which is the formation of the lariat structure via the 2'-5' phosphodiester bond. Therefore, the disruption of the formation of this specific bond is the most direct answer related to the lariat structure. The accumulation implies the *next* step is faulty, but the *formation* of the lariat is the event involving the 2'-5' bond. Let's re-evaluate the options based on this. Option (B) describes the formation of the 2'-5' phosphodiester bond. If this step is defective due to SF3B1 mutation, the lariat would not form or would form inefficiently, and the pre-mRNA might accumulate, or other intermediates might accumulate. However, the vignette *explicitly* states the accumulation of the lariat intermediate. This implies the lariat *was* formed, but the subsequent step failed. The question is poorly phrased if it means "what step is defective *causing* the accumulation". If it means "what molecular event *is* the lariat formation", then (B) is correct. Given the context of USMLE Step 1, questions often focus on the specific biochemical reaction itself. The formation of the lariat involves the 2'-5' phosphodiester bond. A defect in SF3B1 impairs this step. The accumulation of the lariat suggests the first step happened, but the second didn't. However, the *defining* feature of the lariat is the 2'-5' bond. The question asks what is disrupted. The formation of the bond is disrupted by the SF3B1 mutation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Cleavage of the pre-mRNA at the 3' splice site by the U2AF complex. This is the *second* transesterification reaction, occurring *after* lariat formation. A defect here would prevent exon ligation and lariat release, potentially leading to lariat accumulation, but it's not the primary event involving the 2'-5' bond formation. The U2AF complex is involved in recognizing the 3' splice site and catalyzing the second step.
- (C): Adenylylation of the branch point adenosine by the U2 snRNP. This is *not* the correct description of the first step. The branch point adenosine is *not* adenylylated. Instead, its 2'-OH group attacks the 5' splice site phosphate. Adenylylation occurs at the 3' end of the U6 snRNA within the spliceosome, which is important for spliceosome activation, but not directly involved in the lariat formation itself.
- (D): Cleavage of the pre-mRNA at the 5' splice site by the U2 snRNP. The U2 snRNP is involved in the *first* transesterification reaction, but it doesn't cleave the 5' splice site. The 2'-OH of the branch point A attacks the phosphate at the 5' splice site. Cleavage occurs at the 5' splice site during the *second* reaction, catalyzed by U12 snRNP.
- (E): Ligation of the 5' and 3' exons by the RNA ligase. This is the final step of splicing, occurring *after* the second transesterification reaction. A defect here would prevent exon joining, but it's not directly related to the formation of the lariat intermediate or the 2'-5' phosphodiester bond.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The formation of the lariat intermediate is a crucial step in pre-mRNA splicing, involving a 2'-5' phosphodiester bond between the branch point adenosine and the 5' splice site. This reaction is catalyzed by the U2 snRNP complex, which includes the SF3B complex. Mutations in *SF3B1* (encoding a subunit of SF3B) impair this step, leading to aberrant splicing and accumulation of lariat intermediates. This process is distinct from the second transesterification reaction (catalyzed

<a id='question-054'></a>

### Question 054: U1 snRNP Function: Base Pairing with the 5' Splice Donor Site and Initiation of Spliceosome Assembly
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The U1 small nuclear ribonucleoprotein (snRNP) particle recognizes and binds to the 5' splice donor site (GU sequence) at the intron-exon boundary in pre-mRNA, initiating the assembly of the spliceosome complex, which is crucial for proper pre-mRNA splicing. (U1 snRNP 识别并结合到 pre-mRNA 中密码子-外显子边界的 5' 剪接供体位点 (GU 序列)，启动剪接体复合物的组装，这对于正确的 pre-mRNA 剪接至关重要。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents to the genetics clinic with a history of developmental delay, short stature (145 cm), and characteristic facial features including hypertelorism, epicanthal folds, and a low-set jaw. Her parents report that she has always struggled with learning and has difficulty with fine motor skills. Karyotyping reveals a normal female karyotype (46,XX). RNA sequencing of her fibroblasts shows significantly reduced levels of mature mRNA transcripts for several housekeeping genes, including *GAPDH* and *ACTB*. Analysis of pre-mRNA splicing reveals widespread intron retention events. Further investigation reveals a mutation in the *SNRPG* gene, which encodes a protein component of the U1 snRNP complex. Specifically, the mutation affects the interaction between the U1 snRNP and the 5' splice donor site consensus sequence (GU). Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the observed splicing defects in this patient?

(A) Impaired binding of the U2 snRNP to the branch point sequence within the intron.
(B) Failure of the U1 snRNP to recognize and bind to the 5' splice donor site (GU sequence) on the pre-mRNA.
(C) Defective activity of the U5 snRNP, leading to improper base pairing during the spliceosome catalytic steps.
(D) Reduced levels of the U4/U6/U5 tri-snRNP complex, preventing the transition from the A to the B spliceosome activation conformation.
(E) Aberrant function of the U6 snRNA, which acts as a catalytic RNA during the transesterification reactions of splicing.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- The patient presents with developmental delay, short stature, and dysmorphic features, suggesting a genetic syndrome.
- Normal karyotype rules out large chromosomal abnormalities.
- Reduced levels of mature mRNA and widespread intron retention indicate a defect in pre-mRNA splicing.
- The mutation is in the *SNRPG* gene, affecting the U1 snRNP complex.
- The specific effect of the mutation is on the interaction between U1 snRNP and the 5' splice donor site (GU sequence).
- The question asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the splicing defects.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- Pre-mRNA splicing is the process of removing introns and joining exons to form mature mRNA.
- This process is carried out by the spliceosome, a large ribonucleoprotein complex.
- The spliceosome assembly is initiated by the recognition of specific sequences at the intron-exon boundaries.
- The 5' splice site (donor site) typically has a GU dinucleotide sequence.
- The 3' splice site (acceptor site) typically has an AG dinucleotide sequence.
- A branch point sequence (usually containing an adenine) is located within the intron, upstream of the 3' splice site.
- The U1 snRNP is the first component to bind to the pre-mRNA. It recognizes and binds specifically to the 5' splice donor site (GU sequence) through base pairing interactions involving the U1 snRNA (specifically the SmE site).
- This binding event is crucial for initiating the assembly of the spliceosome.
- The U1 snRNP binding recruits other snRNPs (U2, U4/U6/U5 tri-snRNP) to form the spliceosome complex.
- The U2 snRNP then binds to the branch point sequence.
- The U4/U6/U5 tri-snRNP joins the complex.
- The spliceosome undergoes rearrangements (A complex to B complex activation) to become catalytically active.
- The U6 snRNA acts as a catalytic RNA (ribozyme) to perform the two transesterification reactions that remove the intron and ligate the exons.
- In this patient, the mutation in *SNRPG* impairs the interaction between U1 snRNP and the 5' splice donor site. This prevents the initial recognition and binding step.
- Consequently, the spliceosome cannot assemble properly, leading to the failure of intron removal and the accumulation of pre-mRNA with retained introns, resulting in reduced levels of mature mRNA and the observed clinical phenotype.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Impaired binding of the U2 snRNP to the branch point sequence within the intron. This step occurs *after* U1 snRNP binding. If U1 snRNP cannot bind the 5' site, the entire process stalls, and U2 snRNP binding is not initiated. The primary defect here is U1 snRNP binding.
- (C) Defective activity of the U5 snRNP, leading to improper base pairing during the spliceosome catalytic steps. The U5 snRNP is involved in bringing the exons together for ligation during the catalytic steps. While important, the initial recognition and assembly triggered by U1 snRNP binding to the 5' site is the first critical step affected by the described mutation. A defect here would occur later in the spliceosome cycle.
- (D) Reduced levels of the U4/U6/U5 tri-snRNP complex, preventing the transition from the A to the B spliceosome activation conformation. The U4/U6/U5 tri-snRNP is essential for spliceosome activation. However, the vignette specifically states the mutation affects the U1 snRNP interaction with the 5' splice site. While reduced levels of other snRNPs can cause splicing defects, this option doesn't directly address the specific mutation described. The primary defect is the inability of U1 snRNP to initiate binding.
- (E) Aberrant function of the U6 snRNA, which acts as a catalytic RNA during the transesterification reactions of splicing. The U6 snRNA is the catalytic component of the spliceosome, performing the actual cleavage and ligation reactions. This occurs much later in the splicing process, after the spliceosome has assembled. The described mutation affects the initial assembly step mediated by U1 snRNP.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The U1 snRNP is the key initiator of spliceosome assembly by recognizing the 5' splice donor site (GU). Mutations affecting this interaction lead to widespread splicing defects and can cause developmental syndromes. Key differentials for splicing disorders include mutations in other snRNP components (U2, U4, U5, U6), SF3B1 (U2AF component), SRS2 (SF2/Helicase component), and PRPF subunits, each affecting different steps or specific splice sites. Understanding the order of events in spliceosome assembly is crucial.

<a id='question-055'></a>

### Question 055: Splicing Defect: Branch Point Adenosine Recognition
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the molecular mechanism of pre-mRNA splicing, specifically the role of the U2 snRNP in recognizing and binding to the branch point sequence adenosine during spliceosome assembly. (考察剪接过程中的分子机制，特别是U2 snRNP识别并结合分支点序列腺嘌呤的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with developmental delay, hypotonia, and dysmorphic features including hypertelorism, epicanthal folds, and a low-set jaw. His parents are unaffected, but his maternal grandmother had similar, though milder, symptoms. Karyotyping is normal (46,XY). RNA sequencing of his fibroblasts reveals aberrant splicing of multiple pre-mRNAs, including the *SMN1* gene, leading to reduced levels of functional survival motor neuron protein. Analysis of spliceosome components shows a mutation in the *U2AF1* gene, which encodes a subunit of the U2 snRNP complex. Further biochemical studies on the patient's U2 snRNP complex demonstrate a significantly reduced affinity for pre-mRNA sequences containing a branch point adenosine bulging out from the double helix. Which of the following molecular interactions is most likely impaired in this patient's U2 snRNP complex, leading to the observed splicing defects?

(A) Base pairing between the U2 snRNP snRNA and the 5' splice site GU dinucleotide.
(B) Base pairing between the U2 snRNP snRNA and the branch point sequence adenosine.
(C) Base pairing between the U2 snRNP snRNA and the polypyrimidine tract.
(D) Interaction between the U2AF1 subunit and the U1 snRNP complex.
(E) Interaction between the U2 snRNP complex and the 3' splice site.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with developmental delay, hypotonia, dysmorphic features. This suggests a genetic disorder affecting development.
- **Family History:** Unaffected parents, affected maternal grandmother (milder symptoms). This suggests an autosomal dominant inheritance pattern, possibly with variable expressivity or reduced penetrance, or potentially an X-linked dominant pattern (though less likely given unaffected father).
- **Lab Findings:** Karyotype normal (rules out gross chromosomal abnormalities). RNA sequencing shows aberrant splicing of multiple pre-mRNAs (*SMN1* mentioned, relevant to Spinal Muscular Atrophy, but the defect is generalized).
- **Genetic Defect:** Mutation in *U2AF1* gene, encoding a subunit of the U2 snRNP complex. This points directly to a defect in the spliceosome machinery.
- **Biochemical Findings:** Reduced affinity of the patient's U2 snRNP complex for pre-mRNA sequences containing a branch point adenosine bulging out. This is the crucial clue linking the genetic defect to a specific molecular function.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the specific molecular interaction impaired in the patient's U2 snRNP complex.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Pre-mRNA Splicing:** A fundamental process in eukaryotes where introns are removed from pre-mRNA, and exons are joined together to form mature mRNA. This is carried out by the spliceosome, a large ribonucleoprotein complex.
- **Spliceosome Assembly:** The spliceosome assembles on the pre-mRNA in a stepwise manner. Key steps include:
    1.  **U1 snRNP binding:** Recognizes and binds to the 5' splice site (GU dinucleotide) at the beginning of the intron.
    2.  **U2AF binding:** U2 auxiliary factor (U2AF) complex (composed of U2AF1 and U2AF2 subunits) binds to the polypyrimidine tract and the 3' splice site (typically AG dinucleotide) at the end of the intron.
    3.  **U4/U6/U5 tri-snRNP binding:** This complex joins the spliceosome.
    4.  **U1 and U4 release:** U1 snRNP is released from the 5' splice site, and U4 snRNP is released from U6 snRNP.
    5.  **U2 snRNP binding:** The U2 snRNP complex binds to the branch point sequence (BPS) located upstream of the 3' splice site. The BPS contains an adenosine residue (the branch point adenosine) that bulges out of the double helix.
    6.  **Spliceosome Activation:** The spliceosome undergoes rearrangements, activating the catalytic core.
    7.  **Transesterification Reactions:** Two sequential transesterification reactions occur:
        *   The 2'-OH group of the branch point adenosine attacks the phosphate at the 5' splice site, cleaving the pre-mRNA and forming a lariat structure with the intron.
        *   The free 3'-OH group of the 5' exon attacks the phosphate at the 3' splice site, joining the two exons.
    8.  **Spliceosome Disassembly:** The spliceosome disassembles, releasing the ligated mRNA and the intron lariat.
- **Role of U2 snRNP:** The U2 snRNP contains a 150-nucleotide snRNA (U2 snRNA) that is highly conserved. A specific sequence within the U2 snRNA (specifically, nucleotides 19-44) forms a double helix with the branch point sequence (BPS) in the pre-mRNA. This interaction is crucial for positioning the branch point adenosine correctly for the first transesterification reaction. The U2AF1 subunit helps recruit the U2 snRNP to the spliceosome and facilitates this base pairing.
- **Patient's Defect:** The patient has a mutation in *U2AF1* and the U2 snRNP complex has reduced affinity for the branch point adenosine. This directly implicates the interaction between the U2 snRNA and the branch point sequence adenosine.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Base pairing between the U2 snRNP snRNA and the 5' splice site GU dinucleotide. This interaction is mediated by the U1 snRNP, not the U2 snRNP. The U1 snRNA base pairs with the 5' splice site. Therefore, this option is incorrect.
- (C): Base pairing between the U2 snRNP snRNA and the polypyrimidine tract. The polypyrimidine tract is located between the branch point sequence and the 3' splice site. While important for spliceosome assembly and function, the primary interaction of the U2 snRNA is with the branch point sequence adenosine, not the polypyrimidine tract itself. The U2AF complex binds to the polypyrimidine tract and the 3' splice site. Therefore, this option is incorrect.
- (D): Interaction between the U2AF1 subunit and the U1 snRNP complex. U2AF1 is part of the U2AF complex, which binds to the polypyrimidine tract and 3' splice site. While U1 snRNP binds to the 5' splice site, a direct interaction between U2AF1 and U1 snRNP is not the primary mechanism for recognizing the branch point adenosine. The U2 snRNP itself binds to the branch point sequence. Therefore, this option is incorrect.
- (E): Interaction between the U2 snRNP complex and the 3' splice site. The 3' splice site is primarily recognized by the U2AF complex (U2AF1/U2AF2) and later involved in the second transesterification reaction. The U2 snRNP binds to the branch point sequence, which is located upstream of the 3' splice site. Therefore, this option is incorrect.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The U2 snRNP plays a critical role in pre-mRNA splicing by recognizing and binding to the branch point sequence adenosine. This interaction, mediated by base pairing between the U2 snRNA and the branch point sequence, is essential for spliceosome activation and the subsequent transesterification reactions. Defects in U2 snRNP components or their interactions lead to widespread splicing errors and can cause various genetic disorders. Key differentials include defects in other spliceosome components (U1 snRNP, U4/U6 snRNP, U5 snRNP, U2AF complex) or mutations affecting splice site consensus sequences themselves.

<a id='question-056'></a>

### Question 056: Systemic Lupus Erythematosus (SLE) and Anti-Sm Antibodies
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Anti-Smith (Anti-Sm) antibodies are highly specific for Systemic Lupus Erythematosus (SLE) and target core proteins of small nuclear ribonucleoproteins (snRNPs), which are involved in RNA splicing. (Anti-Smith (Anti-Sm) 抗体高度特异于系统性红斑狼疮 (SLE)，其靶标是小核糖核蛋白 (snRNP) 的核心蛋白，参与 RNA 剪接。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old African American woman presents to her primary care physician complaining of fatigue, joint pain (particularly in her hands and wrists), and a malar rash that worsens with sun exposure. She reports that her symptoms have been present for approximately 6 months and are gradually worsening. Her past medical history is unremarkable, and she denies any significant family history of autoimmune diseases. Physical examination reveals a characteristic butterfly-shaped erythematous rash across her cheeks and nose, along with mild swelling and tenderness in her metacarpophalangeal (MCP) and proximal interphalangeal (PIP) joints. Vital signs are normal. Laboratory investigations show a positive antinuclear antibody (ANA) test (titer 1:640, speckled pattern), low complement levels (C3 = 65 mg/dL, normal range 85-190 mg/dL; C4 = 15 mg/dL, normal range 20-50 mg/dL), elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR = 88 mm/hr, normal range 0-20 mm/hr), and proteinuria (3+ on urine dipstick). Further testing reveals the presence of anti-double-stranded DNA (anti-dsDNA) antibodies and anti-Smith (anti-Sm) antibodies. Which of the following molecular targets is specifically recognized by anti-Sm antibodies in this patient?

(A) Histone H1
(B) Ribosomal P proteins
(C) SmB and SmD proteins
(D) Ro (SSA) and La (SSB) proteins
(E) U1 RNP (U1 snRNP) complex

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- The patient presents with classic symptoms of SLE: fatigue, polyarthritis (MCP/PIP joints), malar rash (photosensitive), positive ANA, low complement (C3/C4), elevated ESR, and proteinuria.
- The presence of anti-dsDNA and anti-Sm antibodies strongly supports the diagnosis of SLE.
- Anti-Sm antibodies are highly specific (though not very sensitive) for SLE.
- The question asks for the specific molecular target of anti-Sm antibodies.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- Systemic Lupus Erythematosus (SLE) is a chronic autoimmune disease characterized by the production of autoantibodies against various self-antigens.
- Anti-nuclear antibodies (ANA) are present in >95% of SLE patients. Specific autoantibodies, such as anti-dsDNA and anti-Sm, are more specific for SLE.
- Anti-Sm antibodies target the core proteins of small nuclear ribonucleoproteins (snRNPs).
- snRNPs are complexes of small nuclear RNAs (snRNAs, e.g., U1, U2, U4, U5, U6) and specific proteins.
- These snRNPs are essential components of the spliceosome, the molecular machinery responsible for pre-mRNA splicing in eukaryotes.
- The core proteins of the U1, U2, U4/U6, and U5 snRNPs are Sm proteins (named after the Smith homologous proteins).
- The major Sm proteins are SmB, SmB', SmD1, SmD2, SmD3, SmE, SmF, and SmG.
- Anti-Sm antibodies specifically recognize the SmB, SmB', SmD1, SmD2, SmD3, SmE, SmF, and SmG proteins, which are common to most snRNPs.
- Therefore, anti-Sm antibodies are directed against these core snRNP proteins.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Histone H1: Histone H1 is a core component of nucleosomes, involved in chromatin packaging. Antibodies against histones (e.g., anti-histone antibodies) are commonly found in drug-induced lupus, not typically specific for idiopathic SLE, and are not the target of anti-Sm antibodies.
- (B) Ribosomal P proteins: Antibodies against ribosomal P proteins (anti-P) are associated with SLE, particularly neuropsychiatric lupus, but they are not the target of anti-Sm antibodies. Anti-Sm antibodies target snRNP core proteins.
- (D) Ro (SSA) and La (SSB) proteins: Ro (SSA) and La (SSB) are RNA-binding proteins. Antibodies against Ro/SSA and La/SSB are found in SLE and Sjögren's syndrome. They are associated with photosensitivity, neonatal lupus, and congenital heart block, but they are distinct targets from the snRNP core proteins recognized by anti-Sm antibodies.
- (E) U1 RNP (U1 snRNP) complex: While the U1 snRNP contains Sm proteins (specifically SmB, SmD1, SmD2, SmE, SmF, SmG) and is targeted by anti-Sm antibodies, the U1 RNP complex itself also contains the U1 snRNA and the U1-specific protein U1A. Anti-U1 RNP antibodies target the U1A protein, which is characteristic of Mixed Connective Tissue Disease (MCTD), not specifically SLE (although they can be present in SLE). Anti-Sm antibodies target the *shared* core Sm proteins found in multiple snRNPs, not the U1A protein unique to U1 snRNP. Therefore, while related, targeting the *entire* U1 RNP complex (implying U1A) is incorrect; anti-Sm targets the *core* Sm proteins.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- Anti-Sm antibodies are highly specific for SLE, targeting the core Sm proteins (SmB, SmD1, SmD2, SmE, SmF, SmG) found in snRNPs. Remember this specificity for board exams.
- Differential diagnosis for autoantibodies: Anti-dsDNA (SLE), Anti-histone (Drug-induced lupus), Anti-Ro/SSA & Anti-La/SSB (SLE, Sjögren's), Anti-U1 RNP (MCTD), Anti-P (SLE, neuropsychiatric lupus).

<a id='question-057'></a>

### Question 057: Mixed Connective Tissue Disease (MCTD)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Anti-U1 RNP autoantibodies are the serological hallmark of Mixed Connective Tissue Disease (MCTD), a distinct autoimmune disorder characterized by overlapping features of systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE), systemic sclerosis (SSc), and polymyositis (PM). The autoantibody targets the U1 small nuclear ribonucleoprotein (snRNP) complex.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old woman presents to her primary care physician complaining of Raynaud's phenomenon, swollen hands, and fatigue for the past 6 months. She denies any rash, joint pain, or muscle weakness. Her past medical history is unremarkable, and she takes no medications. Family history is negative for autoimmune diseases. Physical examination reveals puffy fingers, sclerodactyly (tightening of the skin on the fingers), and normal muscle strength. Laboratory tests show a normal complete blood count, comprehensive metabolic panel, and urinalysis. However, an antinuclear antibody (ANA) test is positive at a titer of 1:160. Further serological testing, including anti-dsDNA, anti-Smith (anti-Sm), anti-centromere, anti-Scl-70 (anti-topoisomerase I), and anti-Jo-1 antibodies, are all negative. A specific autoantibody test reveals high titers of anti-U1 RNP antibodies. Based on these findings, which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?

(A) Systemic Lupus Erythematosus (SLE)
(B) Systemic Sclerosis (SSc)
(C) Polymyositis (PM)
(D) Mixed Connective Tissue Disease (MCTD)
(E) Rheumatoid Arthritis (RA)

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 35-year-old woman with Raynaud's phenomenon, swollen hands, fatigue, puffy fingers, and sclerodactyly. These symptoms suggest an autoimmune connective tissue disease.
- **Negative Findings:** Absence of rash, joint pain, muscle weakness. Negative tests for anti-dsDNA, anti-Sm (ruling out SLE), anti-centromere, anti-Scl-70 (ruling out diffuse SSc), and anti-Jo-1 (ruling out PM/polymyositis).
- **Positive Findings:** Positive ANA (1:160), high titers of anti-U1 RNP antibodies.
- **Deduction:** The combination of overlapping features (Raynaud's, swollen hands, sclerodactyly) and the specific presence of high-titer anti-U1 RNP antibodies, along with the exclusion of other specific autoantibodies associated with SLE, SSc, and PM, strongly points towards MCTD. Anti-U1 RNP antibodies are considered the serological hallmark of MCTD.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Anti-U1 RNP Antibodies:** These are autoantibodies directed against the U1 small nuclear ribonucleoprotein (snRNP) complex. The U1 snRNP is composed of a 70-kDa U1A protein (ribonucleoprotein A) and several small nuclear RNAs (snRNAs), including U1 snRNA.
- **MCTD Pathogenesis:** The exact pathogenesis is not fully understood, but it is believed that the anti-U1 RNP antibodies play a role in the inflammatory process and tissue damage seen in MCTD. The antibodies may target intracellular components, leading to cell damage or immune complex formation.
- **Clinical Features of MCTD:** MCTD is characterized by overlapping features of SLE (e.g., arthritis, sometimes rash), SSc (e.g., Raynaud's phenomenon, sclerodactyly, esophageal dysmotility), and PM (e.g., myositis, although often milder than classic PM). The presence of high-titer anti-U1 RNP antibodies is the defining serological feature.
- **Differential Diagnosis:** While anti-U1 RNP antibodies can be found in other conditions like SLE and SSc, high titers are almost exclusively associated with MCTD. The clinical presentation in this vignette, with features overlapping SSc and potentially SLE (though rash and significant joint pain are absent), fits well with MCTD.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Systemic Lupus Erythematosus (SLE): SLE is characterized by a wide range of symptoms, including rash (malar rash, discoid rash), arthritis, serositis, renal involvement, and neurological symptoms. While ANA is positive in most SLE patients, the specific autoantibodies associated with SLE are anti-dsDNA and anti-Sm. Anti-U1 RNP antibodies can be present in SLE (about 30-40% of cases), but they are typically not present in high titers, and anti-dsDNA and/or anti-Sm are usually positive. This patient lacks typical SLE features and is negative for anti-dsDNA and anti-Sm.
- (B) Systemic Sclerosis (SSc): SSc is characterized by fibrosis of the skin and internal organs. Raynaud's phenomenon and sclerodactyly are common features, as seen in this patient. However, specific autoantibodies for SSc include anti-centromere (associated with limited cutaneous SSc) and anti-Scl-70 (anti-topoisomerase I, associated with diffuse cutaneous SSc). This patient is negative for both. While anti-U1 RNP can be seen in SSc, high titers are more characteristic of MCTD.
- (C) Polymyositis (PM): PM is an inflammatory myopathy characterized by proximal muscle weakness. While fatigue can occur, Raynaud's and sclerodactyly are not typical features. The characteristic autoantibody for PM is anti-Jo-1 (part of the anti-synthetase syndrome). This patient has no muscle weakness and is negative for anti-Jo-1.
- (E) Rheumatoid Arthritis (RA): RA is primarily characterized by symmetric polyarthritis, typically affecting small joints of the hands and feet. While fatigue is common, Raynaud's and sclerodactyly are not typical features. The characteristic autoantibodies for RA are rheumatoid factor (RF) and anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide (anti-CCP) antibodies. ANA can be positive, but anti-U1 RNP is not a hallmark.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
High-titer anti-U1 RNP antibodies are the defining serological marker for Mixed Connective Tissue Disease (MCTD), which presents with overlapping features of SLE, SSc, and PM. Remember to differentiate MCTD from SLE (anti-dsDNA, anti-Sm), SSc (anti-centromere, anti-Scl-70), and PM (anti-Jo-1) based on specific autoantibody profiles and clinical presentations.

<a id='question-058'></a>

### Question 058: Beta-Thalassemia Major
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Splice site mutations in the HBB gene can lead to the activation of cryptic splice sites, resulting in aberrant mRNA transcripts and reduced or absent functional β-globin protein synthesis, causing β-thalassemia. (β-珠蛋白基因剪接位点突变可激活隐匿剪接位点，导致异常mRNA转录本和功能性β-珠蛋白减少或缺失，引起β-地中海贫血。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-month-old male infant, born to first-cousin parents of Mediterranean descent, presents with failure to thrive, pallor, and hepatosplenomegaly. His initial hemoglobin level was 6.5 g/dL (normal for age: 11-15 g/dL), and peripheral blood smear shows microcytic, hypochromic red blood cells with marked anisopoikilocytosis and numerous target cells. Hemoglobin electrophoresis reveals significantly reduced HbA (α2β2) and a compensatory increase in HbF (α2γ2) to 80%. Quantitative analysis of globin chain synthesis shows a marked reduction in β-globin chain production (approximately 10% of normal) compared to α-globin chains. Genetic analysis of the patient's DNA reveals a heterozygous mutation in the *HBB* gene. Sequencing of the *HBB* gene reveals a single nucleotide substitution (G→A) at the 3' end of intron 2 (position IVS2+1G>A). This mutation is known to affect splicing. Further analysis of the patient's mRNA using reverse transcriptase PCR (RT-PCR) followed by sequencing shows the presence of a major aberrant transcript lacking exon 3, along with a minor aberrant transcript containing an additional 10 base pairs from intron 2 within exon 3. Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the reduced β-globin synthesis in this patient?

(A) The mutation creates a premature stop codon within exon 3, leading to nonsense-mediated mRNA decay of the transcript.
(B) The mutation alters the promoter sequence of the *HBB* gene, decreasing the rate of transcription initiation.
(C) The mutation creates a cryptic splice donor site within intron 2, leading to the activation of an alternative splice site and the skipping of exon 3.
(D) The mutation creates a cryptic splice acceptor site within exon 3, leading to the activation of an alternative splice site and the inclusion of intron 2 sequence within exon 3.
(E) The mutation introduces a frameshift within exon 3, leading to the production of a truncated, non-functional β-globin protein.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-month-old infant, Mediterranean descent, failure to thrive, pallor, hepatosplenomegaly, microcytic hypochromic anemia, target cells, low HbA, high HbF. This clinical picture is highly suggestive of β-thalassemia major.
- **Lab Findings:** Low HbA, high HbF, reduced β-globin synthesis, normal α-globin synthesis. Confirms a defect specifically in β-globin chain production.
- **Genetic Finding:** Heterozygous mutation in *HBB* gene (IVS2+1G>A). This is a known splice site mutation.
- **mRNA Analysis:** Major aberrant transcript lacking exon 3, minor aberrant transcript with 10 bp intron 2 sequence in exon 3. This indicates abnormal splicing is occurring.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining reduced β-globin synthesis due to the IVS2+1G>A mutation.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- The *HBB* gene encodes the β-globin chain of hemoglobin.
- The mutation is located at the 3' end of intron 2 (IVS2+1G>A). This position is critical for proper splicing.
- The normal splicing process involves the recognition of splice donor sites (GU at the 5' end of introns) and splice acceptor sites (AG at the 3' end of introns) by the spliceosome.
- The G→A substitution at IVS2+1 creates a new, strong splice acceptor site (AG) within intron 2, very close to the actual splice acceptor site at the end of intron 2 (GU at the 5' end of exon 3).
- This new cryptic splice acceptor site (AG) within intron 2 competes with the normal splice acceptor site at the end of intron 2.
- The spliceosome preferentially recognizes the new, stronger cryptic acceptor site within intron 2.
- Splicing occurs at this cryptic site, leading to the inclusion of a portion of intron 2 (approximately 10 base pairs, as seen in the minor aberrant transcript) within the final mRNA molecule.
- This inclusion of intron 2 sequence disrupts the reading frame in exon 3, leading to the production of a non-functional β-globin protein or triggering nonsense-mediated decay (NMD) of the aberrant mRNA.
- The major aberrant transcript lacking exon 3 likely results from the activation of an alternative splice donor site upstream of exon 3, possibly due to the disruption caused by the cryptic acceptor site.
- The net effect is a significant reduction in the production of normal, functional β-globin mRNA and protein, leading to β-thalassemia.
- This mechanism is a classic example of how splice site mutations can cause genetic disease by altering mRNA processing.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): The mutation (IVS2+1G>A) does not create a premature stop codon. Premature stop codons lead to nonsense-mediated mRNA decay (NMD), which can occur in some thalassemia mutations, but this specific mutation primarily affects splicing, not translation termination. The mRNA analysis shows aberrant splicing, not necessarily NMD as the primary mechanism, although NMD might contribute to the reduction of some aberrant transcripts.
- (B): The mutation is located within an intron, specifically at a splice site. While mutations in promoter regions can affect transcription, this mutation's primary effect is on RNA splicing, not transcription initiation rate. The clinical presentation and mRNA analysis point directly to a splicing defect.
- (C): The mutation (IVS2+1G>A) creates a *new splice acceptor site* within intron 2, not a splice donor site. Activation of a cryptic *donor* site would lead to the skipping of an exon or the inclusion of intron sequence *before* an exon, which is not the primary finding described (inclusion of intron 2 sequence *within* exon 3).
- (E): The mutation does not introduce a frameshift within exon 3 itself. A frameshift typically results from an insertion or deletion of nucleotides that is not a multiple of three. While the inclusion of intron 2 sequence *within* exon 3 *can* cause a frameshift, the primary molecular event is the activation of the cryptic splice site leading to this inclusion. Option (D) describes the initiating event (cryptic acceptor site activation) more accurately than (E), which describes a potential consequence of that event. The RT-PCR data specifically shows intron 2 sequence *included* in exon 3, directly supporting the cryptic acceptor site mechanism.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- Splice site mutations are a common cause of genetic disorders, including β-thalassemia. These mutations can create cryptic splice sites (donor or acceptor) or abolish normal splice sites, leading to aberrant mRNA transcripts and reduced protein production.
- Key differential diagnoses for microcytic anemia include iron deficiency anemia, anemia of chronic disease, sideroblastic anemia, and thalassemia. Thalassemia is specifically characterized by impaired globin chain synthesis, often due to mutations affecting transcription, translation, or mRNA processing (splicing).
- Other common β-thalassemia mutations include point mutations causing reduced transcription (e.g., -28A>T), unstable mRNA (e.g., polyadenylation signal mutations), frameshift mutations, nonsense mutations, and splice site mutations. The IVS2+1G>A mutation is one of the most common splice site mutations causing β-thalassemia.

<a id='question-059'></a>

### Question 059: Cystic Fibrosis Splice Site Mutation
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the molecular consequences of splice site mutations, specifically the insertion of a pseudo-exon leading to a frameshift and premature stop codon, resulting in a truncated, non-functional protein in Cystic Fibrosis (CF). (理解剪接位点突变，特别是假外显子插入导致移码和提前终止密码子，从而产生截短的无功能蛋白在囊性纤维化中的分子后果。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 28-year-old male presents to the pulmonary clinic with chronic cough, recurrent respiratory infections, and shortness of breath since childhood. He has a history of pancreatic insufficiency requiring enzyme supplementation. His sweat chloride test was elevated at 85 mEq/L (normal < 40 mEq/L). Genetic testing reveals compound heterozygosity for the CFTR gene. One allele carries the common ΔF508 deletion (c.1521_1523delCTT). Sequencing of the second allele reveals a mutation at nucleotide position 3849+10kbC->T within intron 9. Analysis of mRNA from his peripheral blood leukocytes shows a significantly reduced amount of full-length CFTR transcript and an abnormally sized transcript approximately 10 kb larger than normal. Western blot analysis of cell lysates from his nasal epithelial cells shows a markedly decreased amount of the 170 kDa CFTR protein and the presence of a smaller, unstable protein band around 150 kDa. Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the reduced amount and altered size of the CFTR protein observed in this patient?

(A) The mutation causes a premature termination codon within exon 10, leading to nonsense-mediated mRNA decay (NMD) of the affected transcript and a truncated protein lacking the transmembrane domains.
(B) The mutation creates a novel splice donor site within intron 9, leading to the inclusion of a pseudo-exon derived from the intron sequence, resulting in a frameshift and premature stop codon.
(C) The mutation disrupts the polyadenylation signal, leading to mRNA instability and degradation before translation, resulting in a complete absence of CFTR protein.
(D) The mutation alters the reading frame within exon 10, causing a frameshift that introduces a premature stop codon downstream, leading to a truncated protein lacking the R-domain.
(E) The mutation creates a cryptic splice acceptor site at the 5' end of exon 10, leading to the skipping of the entire exon 10, resulting in a protein lacking the two transmembrane domains encoded by this exon.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** Classic CF symptoms (chronic cough, recurrent infections, shortness of breath, pancreatic insufficiency) and elevated sweat chloride confirm the diagnosis.
- **Genetic Testing:** Compound heterozygote for CFTR. One allele has ΔF508 (a common deletion). The second allele has a mutation at 3849+10kbC->T. This notation indicates a mutation in intron 9, 10 kb upstream of exon 9 (the "+10kb" part), specifically a C to T substitution (3849+10kbC->T).
- **mRNA Analysis:** Reduced full-length CFTR transcript and an abnormally sized transcript (~10 kb larger). This suggests aberrant splicing. The increased size points to the inclusion of intronic sequence.
- **Protein Analysis (Western Blot):** Markedly decreased amount of normal 170 kDa CFTR protein and a smaller, unstable protein (~150 kDa). This indicates that some protein is produced, but it's truncated and likely degraded quickly.
- **Deduction:** The intron mutation (3849+10kbC->T) likely affects splicing. The presence of a larger mRNA transcript suggests the inclusion of intronic sequence (a pseudo-exon). The smaller, truncated protein indicates a frameshift and premature stop codon.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **CFTR Gene Structure:** The CFTR gene contains 27 exons. The mutation 3849+10kbC->T occurs in intron 9, which is located between exons 9 and 10.
- **Splice Site Mutations:** These mutations affect the recognition sites for splicing factors (splice donor site at the 5' end of introns, splice acceptor site at the 3' end of exons).
- **Mechanism of 3849+10kbC->T:** The C->T substitution at the 3849+10kb position within intron 9 creates a new, cryptic splice acceptor site (AG sequence, where the T replaces the C). During splicing, the spliceosome recognizes this new acceptor site instead of the normal one at the end of intron 9.
- **Pseudo-exon Inclusion:** This aberrant recognition leads to the inclusion of a large portion of intron 9 (from the new cryptic site up to the normal splice acceptor site) into the mature mRNA. This included intronic sequence acts as a "pseudo-exon".
- **Frameshift and Premature Stop Codon:** The included intronic sequence is not in the correct reading frame relative to the flanking exons (exon 9 and exon 10). This insertion causes a frameshift. The frameshift continues until a premature stop codon (PTC) is encountered within the pseudo-exon or shortly after the junction with exon 10.
- **mRNA and Protein Consequences:** The resulting mRNA is much larger than normal (~10 kb increase). Translation of this aberrant mRNA produces a truncated CFTR protein. The premature stop codon leads to the synthesis of a protein lacking a significant portion of the C-terminal domain, including the R-domain (regulatory domain). This truncated protein is often unstable and rapidly degraded, explaining the reduced amount of normal CFTR protein detected by Western blot and the presence of a smaller band. This mechanism aligns perfectly with the observed mRNA size increase and the truncated protein product.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): This describes a nonsense mutation (premature stop codon within an exon). While nonsense mutations cause NMD and truncated proteins, the mutation described (3849+10kbC->T) is in an intron and leads to pseudo-exon inclusion, not a premature stop codon within the *exon*. The mRNA size would not increase by 10 kb.
- (C): Mutations disrupting the polyadenylation signal (located in exon 27) lead to mRNA instability and degradation, resulting in a lack of protein. This mutation is in intron 9, far from the polyadenylation signal, and the mRNA is abnormally *large*, not degraded.
- (D): This describes a frameshift mutation within *exon 10*. While it leads to a truncated protein, the mutation site is specified as being in intron 9. Furthermore, a frameshift within exon 10 would not cause the observed ~10 kb increase in mRNA size.
- (E): This describes exon skipping (specifically exon 10). Exon skipping leads to a protein lacking the sequence encoded by the skipped exon. While this can occur due to splice site mutations, the mutation described (3849+10kbC->T) causes *inclusion* of intronic sequence (pseudo-exon), leading to an *increased* mRNA size and a frameshift, not exon skipping. The protein would also be larger, not smaller, if exon 10 were skipped.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **Key Takeaway:** Splice site mutations can lead to aberrant splicing, including exon skipping, intron retention (pseudo-exon inclusion), or use of cryptic splice sites. These alterations often result in frameshifts, premature stop codons, and non-functional or unstable proteins. Understanding the location of the mutation (intron vs. exon) and the specific splicing consequences (mRNA size change, frameshift) is crucial.
- **Differentials:** Compare this mechanism with other CFTR mutations like ΔF508 (protein misfolding/degradation), G551D (gating defect), R117H (chloride conductance defect), or mutations causing exon skipping or nonsense mutations. Each mutation type leads to a different molecular defect and clinical phenotype severity.

<a id='question-060'></a>

### Question 060: Frasier Syndrome: Splice Donor Site Mutation in WT1 Intron 9 Altering +KTS/-KTS Isoform Ratio
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the molecular basis of Frasier Syndrome, specifically how mutations in the *WT1* gene, particularly splice donor site mutations in intron 9, affect the production of the +KTS and -KTS isoforms of the Wilms tumor 1 (WT1) protein, leading to both Wilms tumor and gonadoblastoma. (理解 Frasier 综合征的分子基础，特别是 *WT1* 基因中内含子 9 的剪接供体位点突变如何影响 WT1 蛋白 +KTS 和 -KTS 亚型的产生，导致 Wilms 肿瘤和性腺母细胞瘤。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 4-year-old boy presents to the pediatric oncology clinic with painless abdominal swelling noted by his mother during bath time. His past medical history is unremarkable, and there is no family history of cancer. Physical examination reveals a large, firm, non-tender mass palpable in the left flank, extending below the umbilicus. Vital signs are stable. An abdominal ultrasound confirms a large left renal mass. A nephrectomy is performed, and histological examination reveals a classic triphasic Wilms tumor (nephroblastoma). Genetic testing is ordered due to the patient's young age and the characteristic histology. Simultaneously, the patient undergoes evaluation for potential gonadal abnormalities. A testicular ultrasound reveals bilateral streak gonads with multiple small intratesticular masses. Karyotype analysis of the testicular masses shows a 46,XY mosaicism. Genetic sequencing of the *WT1* gene reveals a heterozygous mutation at the splice donor site of intron 9 (c.1177+1G>A). This mutation is known to affect the splicing of exon 9, which encodes the C-terminal domain (KTS) of the WT1 protein. The patient is subsequently diagnosed with Frasier Syndrome. Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the pathogenesis of Frasier Syndrome in this patient?

(A) The mutation causes a frameshift in exon 9, leading to premature termination of translation and a non-functional truncated WT1 protein, resulting in loss of tumor suppressor function for both the kidney and gonads.
(B) The mutation creates a cryptic splice site within intron 9, leading to the exclusive production of the +KTS isoform, which lacks the nuclear export signal and accumulates in the nucleus, promoting tumorigenesis in both the kidney and gonads.
(C) The mutation disrupts the splice donor site at the beginning of intron 9, leading to the exclusive production of the -KTS isoform, which lacks the nuclear localization signal and is primarily cytoplasmic, resulting in loss of transcriptional repression function necessary for normal kidney and gonad development.
(D) The mutation causes exon skipping of exon 9, leading to the production of a WT1 protein lacking the KTS domain, which results in loss of interaction with the WAGR1/2 complex and promotes Wilms tumor formation, but does not affect gonad development.
(E) The mutation introduces a premature stop codon within exon 9, leading to the production of a dominant-negative WT1 protein that interferes with the function of the wild-type allele, causing both Wilms tumor and gonadoblastoma.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 4-year-old boy with a left renal mass (Wilms tumor). This points towards a genetic predisposition or syndrome associated with Wilms tumor.
- **Gonadal Findings:** Bilateral streak gonads with intratesticular masses (gonadoblastoma) and 46,XY mosaicism. This indicates a disorder of sex development (DSD) affecting the gonads.
- **Genetic Finding:** Heterozygous mutation at the splice donor site of intron 9 in the *WT1* gene (c.1177+1G>A). This mutation specifically affects the splicing of exon 9.
- **Diagnosis:** Frasier Syndrome, characterized by Wilms tumor, gonadoblastoma, and DSD.
- **Question:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the pathogenesis of Frasier Syndrome due to this specific *WT1* mutation.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- The *WT1* gene encodes the Wilms tumor 1 protein, a zinc-finger transcription factor crucial for the development of the kidney and gonads.
- *WT1* undergoes alternative splicing, producing two main isoforms: +KTS (containing the C-terminal domain) and -KTS (lacking the C-terminal domain).
- The +KTS isoform is primarily nuclear, acts as a transcriptional activator, and is essential for normal kidney development and preventing Wilms tumor. It also plays a role in gonad development.
- The -KTS isoform is primarily cytoplasmic, acts as a transcriptional repressor, and is crucial for normal gonad development and preventing gonadoblastoma.
- The C-terminal domain (KTS) contains a nuclear localization signal (NLS) and a nuclear export signal (NES). The +KTS isoform has both, allowing it to shuttle between the nucleus and cytoplasm, but predominantly resides in the nucleus. The -KTS isoform lacks the NLS, hence its cytoplasmic localization.
- The splice donor site mutation (c.1177+1G>A) in intron 9 disrupts the normal splicing process. Intron 9 contains the sequence encoding the KTS domain (exon 9).
- Disruption of the splice donor site at the beginning of intron 9 leads to the skipping of exon 9 during mRNA processing.
- Skipping exon 9 results in the exclusive production of the -KTS isoform.
- The absence of the +KTS isoform leads to impaired kidney development and loss of its tumor suppressor function, predisposing to Wilms tumor.
- The absence of the -KTS isoform leads to impaired gonad development and loss of its tumor suppressor function, predisposing to gonadoblastoma.
- Therefore, the mutation causes a shift in the +KTS/-KTS isoform ratio, favoring the -KTS isoform, which explains the combined phenotype of Wilms tumor and gonadoblastoma seen in Frasier Syndrome.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): A frameshift mutation would lead to a truncated protein, but the specific mutation described (splice donor site) typically causes exon skipping or cryptic splicing, not a frameshift unless it affects the reading frame within the exon itself. Also, the key feature of Frasier Syndrome is the altered *ratio* of isoforms, not complete loss of function due to truncation. The clinical presentation involves *both* Wilms tumor and gonadoblastoma, which is specifically linked to the altered isoform ratio, not just loss of function.
- (B): The mutation described (splice donor site mutation in intron 9) leads to *skipping* of exon 9, resulting in the *exclusive* production of the -KTS isoform, not the +KTS isoform. The +KTS isoform is nuclear due to the NLS in the KTS domain. Exclusive production of +KTS would not explain the phenotype.
- (D): Exon skipping of exon 9 *does* lead to a protein lacking the KTS domain (i.e., the -KTS isoform is produced). However, this option incorrectly states that it does not affect gonad development. The lack of the -KTS isoform is precisely what leads to gonadoblastoma. The correct mechanism involves the *loss* of the +KTS isoform, not the KTS domain itself, leading to Wilms tumor.
- (E): While *WT1* mutations can be dominant-negative, the specific splice donor site mutation described typically leads to exon skipping and an altered isoform ratio, not necessarily a dominant-negative effect or a premature stop codon within the KTS domain. The primary mechanism is the loss of the +KTS isoform function, not a dominant-negative effect.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Frasier Syndrome is caused by heterozygous mutations in the *WT1* gene that disrupt the normal splicing of exon 9, leading to the exclusive production of the -KTS isoform. This results in a loss of +KTS function (predisposing to Wilms tumor) and a loss of -KTS function (predisposing to gonadoblastoma). Key takeaway: Understand the role of *WT1* isoforms (+KTS and -KTS) in kidney and gonad development and tumorigenesis, and how specific mutations affect their production. Differential diagnosis includes other Wilms tumor syndromes (e.g., WAGR, Denys-Drash, Beckwith-Wiedemann) and other DSD syndromes.
