<a id='question-098'></a>

### Question 098: Beta-Thalassemia Major with Splice Site Mutation
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Splice site mutations in the *HBB* gene can lead to the activation of cryptic splice sites, resulting in aberrant mRNA transcripts and reduced or absent functional β-globin protein synthesis, causing β-thalassemia. (β-地中海贫血的剪接位点突变可能激活隐匿的剪接位点，导致异常的 mRNA 转录本，并减少或缺失功能性 β-珠蛋白的合成。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old male of Mediterranean descent presents to the pediatrician with failure to thrive, pallor, and hepatosplenomegaly. His developmental milestones are delayed. Complete blood count (CBC) reveals severe microcytic, hypochromic anemia (Hb 8.5 g/dL, MCV 65 fL, MCH 22 pg). Peripheral blood smear shows marked anisopoikilocytosis, target cells, and nucleated red blood cells. Hemoglobin electrophoresis shows HbF 95%, HbA2 3%, and absent HbA. Genetic testing reveals a heterozygous mutation in the *HBB* gene (c.100+1G>A). RNA sequencing of the patient's peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a major aberrant transcript with an intron retention pattern, resulting in a frameshift and premature stop codon. The patient is started on chronic red blood cell transfusions and iron chelation therapy. Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the pathogenesis of this patient's β-thalassemia?

(A) Nonsense mutation leading to premature termination of translation of the β-globin mRNA.
(B) Frameshift mutation due to a deletion of a single nucleotide in the coding sequence of the *HBB* gene.
(C) Activation of a cryptic splice site within intron 1 of the *HBB* gene, leading to the inclusion of intron sequence in the mature mRNA.
(D) Promoter mutation resulting in decreased transcription of the *HBB* gene.
(E) Polyadenylation site mutation leading to mRNA instability and degradation.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old Mediterranean male with failure to thrive, pallor, hepatosplenomegaly, developmental delay, severe microcytic hypochromic anemia, anisopoikilocytosis, target cells, nucleated RBCs. This clinical picture is classic for severe β-thalassemia major.
*   **Hemoglobin Electrophoresis:** HbF 95%, HbA2 3%, absent HbA. This confirms the absence of functional adult hemoglobin (HbA), characteristic of β-thalassemia major. The compensatory increase in HbF is typical.
*   **Genetic Testing:** Heterozygous mutation in the *HBB* gene (c.100+1G>A). This indicates a mutation in the *HBB* gene, but the specific location is crucial. The notation "c.100+1G>A" refers to the first nucleotide (+1) after exon 1 (intron 1), specifically position 100 relative to the transcription start site. This location is within an intron.
*   **RNA Sequencing:** Major aberrant transcript with intron retention, frameshift, and premature stop codon. This is the key finding. The mutation (c.100+1G>A) likely disrupts the normal splice donor site at the end of exon 1 (or the splice acceptor site at the beginning of exon 2, depending on the exact canonical site). This disruption leads to the activation of a *cryptic* splice site downstream within intron 1. Splicing occurs at this cryptic site, incorporating a portion of intron 1 into the mRNA. This intron retention causes a frameshift, leading to a premature stop codon and a non-functional β-globin protein.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the pathogenesis.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Normal Splicing:** Eukaryotic genes contain exons (coding regions) and introns (non-coding regions). Pre-mRNA undergoes splicing to remove introns and join exons. This process is mediated by the spliceosome, which recognizes specific sequences at the exon-intron junctions (splice donor site at the 3' end of an intron, splice acceptor site at the 5' end of an intron).
*   **Splice Site Mutations:** Mutations can occur at these splice sites, disrupting normal splicing.
    *   **Canonical Site Mutations:** Mutations directly within the consensus splice donor (GT) or acceptor (AG) sequences can impair spliceosome recognition.
    *   **Cryptic Site Activation:** Mutations near canonical sites can weaken their recognition, leading to the activation of alternative, "cryptic" splice sites located elsewhere in the pre-mRNA (often within introns).
*   **Mechanism in this Case:** The mutation c.100+1G>A is located in intron 1, near the junction between exon 1 and intron 1. This mutation likely disrupts the normal splice donor site at the 3' end of exon 1. The spliceosome, unable to efficiently recognize the canonical site, may instead recognize a cryptic splice donor site located further downstream within intron 1. Splicing at this cryptic site would lead to the inclusion of a significant portion of intron 1 sequence in the mature mRNA.
*   **Consequences:** Inclusion of intron sequence causes a frameshift in the reading frame downstream of the splice site. This frameshift alters the amino acid sequence and often introduces a premature stop codon, leading to the production of a truncated, non-functional β-globin protein or triggering nonsense-mediated mRNA decay. The RNA sequencing results confirm this mechanism (intron retention, frameshift, premature stop codon).
*   **Beta-Thalassemia:** Reduced or absent functional β-globin chains lead to ineffective erythropoiesis, hemolysis, and the clinical features of β-thalassemia.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Nonsense mutation leading to premature termination of translation:** While premature termination occurs in this case, it is a *consequence* of the frameshift caused by intron retention, not the primary mechanism. A direct nonsense mutation (e.g., a point mutation changing a codon to a stop codon) would occur within an exon, not be caused by a splice site mutation in an intron. This option describes a different type of mutation.
*   **(B) Frameshift mutation due to a deletion of a single nucleotide in the coding sequence:** A frameshift mutation *does* occur, but it is caused by the *inclusion* of intron sequence due to aberrant splicing, not by a deletion within the coding sequence (exon). This option describes a different type of mutation.
*   **(D) Promoter mutation resulting in decreased transcription:** Promoter mutations affect the rate of transcription initiation, leading to reduced mRNA levels. While some β-thalassemia cases are caused by promoter mutations (e.g., -28 A>G mutation), the genetic finding here (c.100+1G>A) is within an intron, and the RNA sequencing shows an aberrant transcript, not simply reduced levels of normal transcript. This option is inconsistent with the provided genetic and RNA data.
*   **(E) Polyadenylation site mutation leading to mRNA instability and degradation:** Polyadenylation site mutations affect the addition of the poly(A) tail, which is crucial for mRNA stability, export, and translation. While this can cause reduced protein levels, the key finding here is the presence of an *aberrant* mRNA transcript with intron retention, which is characteristic of a splice site defect, not a polyadenylation defect.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Splice site mutations are a common cause of genetic disease, including β-thalassemia. These mutations can disrupt canonical splice sites or activate cryptic splice sites, leading to aberrant mRNA processing (e.g., exon skipping, intron retention, use of alternative polyadenylation sites). The consequences often include frameshifts, premature stop codons, and reduced or absent functional protein. Key differentials for β-thalassemia include α-thalassemia, sickle cell disease, and other hemoglobinopathies. Understanding the molecular basis of these disorders is crucial for diagnosis and genetic counseling.

---

<a id='question-099'></a>

### Question 099: Cystic Fibrosis Splice Site Mutation
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the molecular consequences of splice site mutations in the *CFTR* gene, specifically the insertion of a pseudo-exon leading to a premature stop codon and subsequent protein degradation, and how this relates to clinical presentation and diagnostic findings. (理解囊性纤维化 *CFTR* 基因剪接位点突变，特别是伪外显子插入导致提前终止密码子和后续蛋白质降解的分子后果，以及这如何与临床表现和诊断结果相关。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 28-year-old male presents to the pulmonology clinic with a chronic productive cough, recurrent respiratory infections (including *Pseudomonas aeruginosa* and *Staphylococcus aureus*), and exertional dyspnea since childhood. He also reports steatorrhea and difficulty gaining weight despite a normal appetite. His sweat chloride test result is elevated at 95 mEq/L (normal < 40 mEq/L). Genetic testing reveals a compound heterozygous mutation in the *CFTR* gene. One allele carries a known deletion of phenylalanine at position 508 (ΔF508). Sequencing of the second allele reveals a mutation at nucleotide position 3849+10kbC->T within intron 9. Analysis of mRNA from the patient's peripheral blood leukocytes shows a transcript that is significantly larger than the normal *CFTR* mRNA (~4.5 kb) and contains a premature stop codon within the inserted sequence. Western blot analysis of epithelial cells derived from the patient's nasal brushings shows a complete absence of the CFTR protein.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the absence of functional CFTR protein in this patient?

(A) The mutation causes a frameshift, leading to a truncated protein lacking the ATP-binding domain.
(B) The mutation introduces a premature stop codon in exon 10, resulting in a non-functional protein that is rapidly degraded by the proteasome.
(C) The mutation activates cryptic splice sites, leading to the insertion of a pseudo-exon derived from intron 9 into the mRNA transcript, which contains a premature stop codon and results in nonsense-mediated mRNA decay (NMD) and/or degradation of the resulting truncated protein.
(D) The mutation alters the glycosylation pattern of the CFTR protein, preventing its proper trafficking to the apical membrane.
(E) The mutation leads to misfolding of the CFTR protein in the endoplasmic reticulum, triggering endoplasmic reticulum-associated degradation (ERAD).

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** Chronic respiratory symptoms (cough, infections, dyspnea), gastrointestinal symptoms (steatorrhea, poor weight gain), elevated sweat chloride – classic signs of Cystic Fibrosis (CF).
*   **Genetic Testing:** Compound heterozygosity for *CFTR* mutations. One allele has ΔF508 (a common severe mutation). The second allele has a splice site mutation (3849+10kbC->T).
*   **Molecular Analysis:**
    *   mRNA analysis shows a larger-than-normal transcript. This indicates the inclusion of intronic sequence (a pseudo-exon).
    *   The larger transcript contains a premature stop codon. This means the reading frame is maintained until this point, but the protein synthesis terminates early.
    *   Western blot shows absent CFTR protein. This indicates the mRNA is either degraded or translated into a non-functional, rapidly degraded protein.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the lack of functional CFTR protein.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **CFTR Gene & Protein:** The *CFTR* gene encodes the Cystic Fibrosis Transmembrane conductance Regulator protein, a chloride channel crucial for epithelial transport. Mutations lead to CF.
*   **Splice Site Mutations:** These mutations affect the normal splicing process (removal of introns, joining of exons). The mutation 3849+10kbC->T occurs in intron 9. The "+10kb" indicates the position relative to the 3' end of the intron (or the start of the next exon, exon 10). The C->T change likely disrupts the splice donor site (5' splice site) of the adjacent exon 10 or creates a new cryptic splice acceptor site within intron 9.
*   **Mechanism of 3849+10kbC->T:** This specific mutation is known to activate a cryptic splice acceptor site within intron 9. This leads to the incorrect splicing of the pre-mRNA, causing a portion of intron 9 (the "pseudo-exon") to be included in the final mRNA transcript.
*   **Consequences of Pseudo-exon Insertion:**
    *   **Larger mRNA:** The inclusion of the pseudo-exon increases the size of the mRNA transcript.
    *   **Premature Stop Codon:** The pseudo-exon sequence contains a stop codon (TAA, TAG, or TGA). This leads to premature termination of translation.
    *   **Truncated Protein:** Translation stops early, producing a truncated CFTR protein.
    *   **Nonsense-Mediated mRNA Decay (NMD):** The presence of a premature stop codon within the mRNA often triggers NMD, a cellular surveillance pathway that degrades the aberrant mRNA, preventing the production of potentially harmful truncated proteins.
    *   **Protein Degradation:** Even if the mRNA escapes NMD and is translated, the resulting truncated protein is often unstable and rapidly degraded by cellular proteases (e.g., the proteasome).
*   **Overall Effect:** The net result is a severe reduction or complete absence of functional CFTR protein at the cell surface, leading to the clinical manifestations of CF.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) The mutation causes a frameshift, leading to a truncated protein lacking the ATP-binding domain.** Frameshift mutations involve insertions or deletions of nucleotides that are not multiples of three, altering the reading frame. While this can lead to premature stop codons and truncated proteins, the 3849+10kbC->T mutation is a point mutation (substitution) within an intron, not an insertion or deletion. It affects splicing, not the reading frame directly. Therefore, this mechanism is incorrect for this specific mutation.
*   **(B) The mutation introduces a premature stop codon in exon 10, resulting in a non-functional protein that is rapidly degraded by the proteasome.** While premature stop codons *do* lead to non-functional proteins often degraded by the proteasome, this mutation occurs in *intron 9* and affects *splicing*. It does not directly introduce a stop codon in exon 10. The stop codon is introduced *within the pseudo-exon* derived from the intron. Therefore, this description is inaccurate for the mechanism of this specific splice site mutation.
*   **(D) The mutation alters the glycosylation pattern of the CFTR protein, preventing its proper trafficking to the apical membrane.** Some CFTR mutations (like ΔF508) affect protein folding and trafficking, often involving defects in glycosylation or ERAD. However, the primary mechanism of a splice site mutation like 3849+10kbC->T is the inclusion of a pseudo-exon and subsequent premature termination or mRNA decay, not altered glycosylation.
*   **(E) The mutation leads to misfolding of the CFTR protein in the endoplasmic reticulum, triggering endoplasmic reticulum-associated degradation (ERAD).** Misfolding is a common consequence of mutations like ΔF508, leading to ERAD. However, the 3849+10kbC->T mutation primarily affects splicing and mRNA stability/translation termination, not the intrinsic folding of the protein itself (although the truncated protein produced would certainly be non-functional and likely degraded). The core defect is the aberrant splicing event.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Understanding the molecular consequences of different types of mutations in the *CFTR* gene is crucial. Splice site mutations can lead to exon skipping, intron retention, or pseudo-exon insertion, often resulting in frameshifts, premature stop codons, and subsequent mRNA decay or truncated protein degradation. This contrasts with missense mutations (amino acid change), nonsense mutations (premature stop codon within an exon), frameshift mutations (insertions/deletions), or mutations affecting protein folding/trafficking (like ΔF508). Key differentials include mutations causing protein misfolding (ΔF508, G551D) leading to ERAD, gating mutations affecting channel function, and mutations affecting mRNA stability or processing (like this splice site mutation).

---

<a id='question-100'></a>

### Question 100: Frasier Syndrome: Splice Donor Site Mutation in WT1 Intron 9 Altering +KTS/-KTS Isoform Ratio - Clinical Scenario 3
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the molecular basis of Frasier Syndrome, specifically how mutations in the *WT1* gene, particularly splice donor site mutations in intron 9, affect the production of the +KTS and -KTS isoforms, leading to altered gonadal development and increased risk of Wilms tumor and gonadoblastoma. (理解 Frasier 综合征的分子基础，特别是 *WT1* 基因中内含子 9 的剪接供体位点突变如何影响 +KTS 和 -KTS 同工酶的产生，导致性腺发育异常以及 Wilms 肿瘤和性腺母细胞瘤的风险增加。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-year-old phenotypic female is brought to the pediatrician by her parents due to concerns about her delayed puberty and ambiguous genitalia noted since birth. Physical examination reveals bilateral streak gonads, short stature (110 cm, 3rd percentile), and a broad chest. Karyotype analysis shows a 46,XY female phenotype. Abdominal ultrasound reveals a 7 cm left renal mass. Laboratory investigations show elevated serum levels of anti-Müllerian hormone (AMH) and decreased levels of inhibin B. Genetic testing reveals a heterozygous mutation in the splice donor site of intron 9 of the *WT1* gene. Further molecular analysis of the patient's kidney biopsy shows overexpression of the *WT1* transcript lacking exon 9 (+KTS isoform) and reduced expression of the *WT1* transcript containing exon 9 (-KTS isoform).

Which of the following molecular mechanisms BEST explains the pathogenesis of the patient's condition?

(A) The mutation causes a frameshift in the *WT1* gene, leading to a premature stop codon and a non-functional protein, resulting in impaired kidney development and gonadal differentiation.
(B) The mutation leads to the loss of the zinc finger domain in the WT1 protein, impairing its DNA binding ability and transcription factor activity, which is crucial for both kidney and gonadal development.
(C) The mutation disrupts the normal splicing process, leading to the preferential inclusion of exon 9, resulting in an excess of the -KTS isoform. This isoform lacks the nuclear localization signal and cannot properly regulate genes involved in kidney and gonadal development.
(D) The mutation disrupts the normal splicing process, leading to the preferential exclusion of exon 9, resulting in an excess of the +KTS isoform. This isoform lacks the nuclear localization signal and cannot properly regulate genes involved in kidney and gonadal development.
(E) The mutation causes a missense mutation in the AT-hook domain of the WT1 protein, impairing its interaction with chromatin and leading to dysregulation of genes involved in cell cycle control and apoptosis, contributing to tumor formation.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 6-year-old phenotypic female, 46,XY karyotype, ambiguous genitalia, bilateral streak gonads, short stature, broad chest. This points towards a disorder of sex development (DSD) in a 46,XY individual, specifically gonadal dysgenesis.
- **Renal Mass:** 7 cm left renal mass suggests Wilms tumor, a known complication associated with certain genetic syndromes affecting kidney development.
- **Lab Findings:** Elevated AMH (produced by Sertoli cells, present in streak gonads) and decreased inhibin B (produced by granulosa cells, absent due to lack of functional ovaries) are consistent with gonadal dysgenesis in a 46,XY individual.
- **Genetic Finding:** Heterozygous mutation in the splice donor site of intron 9 of the *WT1* gene. This indicates a defect in the splicing process of the *WT1* pre-mRNA.
- **Molecular Analysis:** Overexpression of the +KTS isoform (lacking exon 9) and reduced expression of the -KTS isoform (containing exon 9). This confirms the mutation affects the splicing of exon 9.
- **Connecting the dots:** The clinical picture (DSD, Wilms tumor), genetic finding (*WT1* splice site mutation in intron 9), and molecular analysis (altered +KTS/-KTS ratio) all point towards Frasier Syndrome. The question asks for the mechanism linking the mutation to the phenotype.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **WT1 Gene and Protein:** The *WT1* gene encodes the Wilms tumor 1 protein, a zinc finger transcription factor crucial for the development of the kidney and gonads. It exists in two main isoforms: +KTS (containing the C-terminal KTS domain) and -KTS (lacking the KTS domain).
- **Isoform Function:** The -KTS isoform is primarily nuclear and acts as a transcriptional activator, regulating genes essential for kidney and gonadal development. The +KTS isoform is predominantly cytoplasmic and acts as a transcriptional repressor, involved in cell cycle control and apoptosis.
- **Splicing Regulation:** The inclusion or exclusion of exon 9, which encodes the KTS domain, is regulated by alternative splicing. The splice donor site mutation in intron 9 disrupts this process.
- **Frasier Syndrome Pathogenesis:** In Frasier Syndrome, mutations affecting the splice donor site in intron 9 typically lead to the *exclusion* of exon 9 during splicing. This results in an excess of the +KTS isoform and a deficiency of the -KTS isoform.
- **Consequences of Altered Ratio:** The lack of sufficient -KTS isoform (the main transcriptional activator for kidney and gonadal development) leads to impaired development of these organs, resulting in gonadal dysgenesis (streak gonads in 46,XY individuals) and an increased risk of Wilms tumor. The excess +KTS isoform may contribute to tumor formation by repressing tumor suppressor genes or promoting cell proliferation. The clinical presentation described (46,XY DSD, Wilms tumor, *WT1* intron 9 splice site mutation, increased +KTS/decreased -KTS ratio) is characteristic of Frasier Syndrome caused by this specific type of mutation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** While *WT1* mutations can cause frameshifts and premature stop codons, leading to non-functional protein and impaired development, this specific mutation is a splice site mutation in intron 9. The molecular analysis shows altered isoform ratios, not a complete loss of function due to a truncated protein. The mechanism described here doesn't fit the specific molecular findings (altered splicing of exon 9).
- (B): **Incorrect.** Loss of the zinc finger domain would impair DNA binding and transcription factor activity, which is a mechanism seen in some *WT1* mutations. However, this specific mutation affects splicing of exon 9, not the zinc finger domain itself. The molecular analysis points to altered isoform ratios, not a defect in the DNA-binding domain.
- (C): **Incorrect.** This option describes the *opposite* effect of the mutation. The mutation leads to the *exclusion* of exon 9, resulting in an *excess* of the +KTS isoform and a *reduction* of the -KTS isoform. This option incorrectly states preferential *inclusion* of exon 9 leading to excess -KTS.
- (E): **Incorrect.** While the +KTS isoform does play a role in cell cycle control and apoptosis, and mutations in *WT1* can contribute to tumor formation, this option focuses on a missense mutation in the AT-hook domain. The vignette describes a splice donor site mutation in intron 9 affecting exon 9 inclusion, leading to altered isoform ratios, not a missense mutation in the AT-hook domain.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Frasier Syndrome is caused by heterozygous mutations in the *WT1* gene, often affecting splicing (like the intron 9 splice donor site mutation described). These mutations typically lead to an altered ratio of the +KTS and -KTS isoforms, with exclusion of exon 9 resulting in excess +KTS and deficiency of -KTS. This disrupts kidney and gonadal development, causing 46,XY DSD (gonadal dysgenesis) and increasing the risk of Wilms tumor. Key differential diagnoses for 46,XY DSD include Swyer syndrome (*SOX9* mutations), androgen insensitivity syndrome (*AR* mutations), and 5-alpha-reductase deficiency (*SRD5A2* mutations). Understanding the specific molecular consequences of different *WT1* mutations is crucial.
